========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s... I guess the short answer is that mostly, I just don't feel like myself anymore. But it’s not really a short answer kind of thing, because it’s just been this heavy, slow change. I used to be a guy who was always moving. You know, you get used to a certain pace in my line of work, always being on alert, always doing something. And outside of that, I was really active. I’d go hiking up the ridge every single weekend, rain or shine, just to clear my head. I was coaching a youth league, which takes a ton of energy, planning the drills and talking to the parents. I’d come home and play my guitar—this old acoustic I’ve had since I got my bachelor's—just to unwind. 

But now... I don't know. I just stopped. I stopped hiking completely. I dropped out of coaching, didn't even really give them a good excuse. I haven't picked up the guitar in months, maybe longer, the strings are probably dead by now. I don't even call people back anymore. My phone will buzz, and I’ll look at the name, and I just... I can't do it. I just let it ring and tell myself I'll do it later, but I never do. 

And the worst part is, some days I just sit in that small rental of mine for hours. Just sitting there in the quiet. I'll be looking at the wall or the floor, and I know there are things to do. I have a whole list in my head of things I should be doing, basic things like laundry or taking out the trash or getting groceries, but I just can't make myself start. It’s like the connection between my brain saying "get up" and my body actually moving is just completely severed. I just sit there while the whole day disappears, and I can't figure out where the time went or where the old me went.
=====================================

Well, it’s... it’s definitely been longer than a few weeks. It’s not one of those things where you just wake up one Tuesday and everything is different, you know? It’s more like... like a slow leak in a tire. You don't notice it at first, but eventually, you're just riding on the rim. 

If I really sit down and trace it back, I think things really started unraveling around the time I moved into this smaller rental. That was... gosh, that was right after the divorce was finalized. So, we're talking about a year and two months ago, roughly. Give or take a couple of weeks. 

Moving is always stressful, right? Especially when it's under those circumstances. You're packing up a whole life, dividing things into boxes, trying to figure out what fits in a place that's half the size of what you're used to. I thought the exhaustion was just from all that. Just the physical toll of hauling furniture up a flight of stairs by myself, because I didn't want to bother anyone to help. And then the emotional toll of it all. I kept telling myself, "Once the boxes are unpacked, once I get the pictures hung up and find a place for everything, I'll bounce back." 

But I never really unpacked everything. There are still boxes sitting in the corner of the bedroom. I just... I ran out of gas. And that was over a year ago. So yeah, it’s been a long time. A year and two months of just... fading out.

Well, that's the thing. I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? Because you'd think, over the course of fourteen months, there'd be at least a weekend where I woke up and thought, "Hey, I'm going to go tackle that trail today," or, "I'm going to finally restring that acoustic and learn a new song." You'd think there'd be a spike, even a small one. 

But honestly, if I'm looking back at the whole stretch since I moved into that rental... it's just been this flat, gray line. It's not like a roller coaster where I have a good week and then crash for a bad week. It's more like I just sank to the bottom of a pool and I've just been walking along the bottom ever since. I mean, sure, maybe there's a Tuesday where I manage to actually take the trash out before it piles up too high, or a Thursday where I microwave a meal instead of just eating cold cereal, but I wouldn't call that feeling *better*. I wouldn't call that feeling like my old self. 

My old self would have been out there organizing a neighborhood watch or running drills with the kids until the sun went down. My old self wouldn't consider taking out the trash a major victory for the week. So no, there haven't really been any stretches where I bounced back or felt the fog lift. It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. It's just been a solid year and change of that same, exact nothingness.

It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, you try to think back over forty-five years of life and you try to find a precedent for something like this, right? Because you want to tell yourself, oh, I beat this before, I know the playbook, I can do it again. But honestly... no. 

I mean, sure, everyone gets tired. When I was getting my bachelor's, I was working nights to pay tuition, studying during the day, barely sleeping. I was exhausted. Bone-tired. And later on, pulling double shifts on the force... you get drained. In my line of work, you see a lot of heavy things, you deal with high-stress situations where you have to be completely dialed in because people are relying on you to keep them safe. You come home from a shift like that and you're wiped out. But that was always a completely different kind of tired. It was a physical tired. I still cared. I still had that drive to get up the next day, put the uniform on, and get back out there. 

Even when things got really rough personally, like when my ex-wife and I first started having real problems, before the divorce actually happened... it was incredibly stressful. It kept me up at night, my stomach would be in knots, but I never lost my engine. I never lost that spark that makes you get up and actually participate in your own life. If I had ever had a month, or even a couple of weeks, where I couldn't get off the couch or couldn't bring myself to care about basic hygiene or my responsibilities, they would have noticed at work immediately. You can't hide that kind of thing when you're working protective services. They would have pulled me off the street so fast my head would spin. 

So before this whole mess started a year and two months ago? Never. I've never felt this heavy, empty feeling in my entire life. I was always the guy who pushed through, the guy who stayed busy to fix the problem. This complete lack of... of everything... it's completely new territory for me. I keep searching my memory trying to find a time I felt even a fraction of this, just to make it make sense, but there's nothing. I've never been here before.

I just sit there while the whole day disappears, and I can't figure out where the time went or where the old me went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s definitely been longer than a few weeks. It’s not one of those things where you just wake up one Tuesday and everything is different, you know? It’s more like... like a slow leak in a tire. You don't notice it at first, but eventually, you're just riding on the rim. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back, I think things really started unraveling around the time I moved into this smaller rental. That was... gosh, that was right after the divorce was finalized. So, we're talking about a year and two months ago, roughly. Give or take a couple of weeks. \n\nMoving is always stressful, right? Especially when it's under those circumstances. You're packing up a whole life, dividing things into boxes, trying to figure out what fits in a place that's half the size of what you're used to. I thought the exhaustion was just from all that. Just the physical toll of hauling furniture up a flight of stairs by myself, because I didn't want to bother anyone to help. And then the emotional toll of it all. I kept telling myself, \"Once the boxes are unpacked, once I get the pictures hung up and find a place for everything, I'll bounce back.\" \n\nBut I never really unpacked everything. There are still boxes sitting in the corner of the bedroom. I just... I ran out of gas. And that was over a year ago. So yeah, it’s been a long time. A year and two months of just... fading out.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year and two months, has your mood and energy stayed pretty much the same low level the whole time, or have there been certain stretches—days or weeks—where you actually felt noticeably better or more like your old self, followed by times it got worse again?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing. I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? Because you'd think, over the course of fourteen months, there'd be at least a weekend where I woke up and thought, \"Hey, I'm going to go tackle that trail today,\" or, \"I'm going to finally restring that acoustic and learn a new song.\" You'd think there'd be a spike, even a small one. \n\nBut honestly, if I'm looking back at the whole stretch since I moved into that rental... it's just been this flat, gray line. It's not like a roller coaster where I have a good week and then crash for a bad week. It's more like I just sank to the bottom of a pool and I've just been walking along the bottom ever since. I mean, sure, maybe there's a Tuesday where I manage to actually take the trash out before it piles up too high, or a Thursday where I microwave a meal instead of just eating cold cereal, but I wouldn't call that feeling *better*. I wouldn't call that feeling like my old self. \n\nMy old self would have been out there organizing a neighborhood watch or running drills with the kids until the sun went down. My old self wouldn't consider taking out the trash a major victory for the week. So no, there haven't really been any stretches where I bounced back or felt the fog lift. It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. It's just been a solid year and change of that same, exact nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Before this stretch that started with your divorce and move, had you ever gone through a separate period—at any earlier point in your life—where you felt this same kind of low, heavy, no-energy state for weeks or more, followed by a return to feeling normal again?\n\nPatient: Well, I... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, you try to think back over forty-five years of life and you try to find a precedent for something like this, right? Because you want to tell yourself, oh, I beat this before, I know the playbook, I can do it again. But honestly... no. \n\nI mean, sure, everyone gets tired. When I was getting my bachelor's, I was working nights to pay tuition, studying during the day, barely sleeping. I was exhausted. Bone-tired. And later on, pulling double shifts on the force... you get drained. In my line of work, you see a lot of heavy things, you deal with high-stress situations where you have to be completely dialed in because people are relying on you to keep them safe. You come home from a shift like that and you're wiped out. But that was always a completely different kind of tired. It was a physical tired. I still cared. I still had that drive to get up the next day, put the uniform on, and get back out there. \n\nEven when things got really rough personally, like when my ex-wife and I first started having real problems, before the divorce actually happened... it was incredibly stressful. It kept me up at night, my stomach would be in knots, but I never lost my engine. I never lost that spark that makes you get up and actually participate in your own life. If I had ever had a month, or even a couple of weeks, where I couldn't get off the couch or couldn't bring myself to care about basic hygiene or my responsibilities, they would have noticed at work immediately. You can't hide that kind of thing when you're working protective services. They would have pulled me off the street so fast my head would spin. \n\nSo before this whole mess started a year and two months ago? Never. I've never felt this heavy, empty feeling in my entire life. I was always the guy who pushed through, the guy who stayed busy to fix the problem. This complete lack of... of everything... it's completely new territory for me. I keep searching my memory trying to find a time I felt even a fraction of this, just to make it make sense, but there's nothing. I've never been here before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If I had ever had a month, or even a couple of weeks, where I couldn't get off the couch or couldn't bring myself to care about basic hygiene or my responsibilities, they would have noticed at work immediately. You can't hide that kind of thing when you're working protective services. They would have pulled me off the street so fast my head would spin. \n\nSo before this whole mess started a year and two months ago? Never. I've never felt this heavy, empty feeling in my entire life. I was always the guy who pushed through, the guy who stayed busy to fix the problem. This complete lack of... of everything... it's completely new territory for me. I keep searching my memory trying to find a time I felt even a fraction of this, just to make it make sense, but there's nothing. I've never been here before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this past year, have you had any thoughts of death, or thoughts that you'd be better off not being here?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / protective_service_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... no. Not really. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around making plans or... or thinking about hurting myself or anything like that. That's just... that's not how I operate. Even with all this, even with the divorce and the move and just feeling like I'm made of lead most days, I haven't gone down that road. 

You know, in my line of work, you see the aftermath of that kind of thing. You see what it does to the people left behind. The families, the kids. I've had to be the one to secure a scene where someone... where someone just couldn't take it anymore. And you stand there in that room, and you see the absolute devastation, the permanence of it. It's a heavy thing to carry, seeing that. And I think, because I've seen it up close, because I know exactly what that looks like and smells like and feels like... it's just not an option in my brain. It's like a door that's permanently locked. 

Sure, I wake up and I stare at that water stain on the ceiling and I think, "God, I don't want to do this today. I don't want to drag myself out of this bed and go through the motions of another empty day." I definitely feel that. I feel the exhaustion of just existing right now. But wanting to actually be dead? Wanting to end it? No. 

I just want... I want the heavy feeling to stop. I want to care about things again. I want to look at my guitar and actually want to pick it up, instead of just seeing it as another thing collecting dust. I want to feel like the guy who used to run drills and organize the neighborhood, you know? I don't want to be gone. I just want to be *me* again. So, no... no thoughts of death. Just thoughts of... of wanting to find my way back, I guess, even if I have no idea how to do that right now.

I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? Because you'd think, over the course of fourteen months, there'd be at least a weekend where I woke up and thought, \"Hey, I'm going to go tackle that trail today,\" or, \"I'm going to finally restring that acoustic and learn a new song.\" You'd think there'd be a spike, even a small one. \n\nBut honestly, if I'm looking back at the whole stretch since I moved into that rental... it's just been this flat, gray line. It's not like a roller coaster where I have a good week and then crash for a bad week. It's more like I just sank to the bottom of a pool and I've just been walking along the bottom ever since. I mean, sure, maybe there's a Tuesday where I manage to actually take the trash out before it piles up too high, or a Thursday where I microwave a meal instead of just eating cold cereal, but I wouldn't call that feeling *better*. I wouldn't call that feeling like my old self. \n\nMy old self would have been out there organizing a neighborhood watch or running drills with the kids until the sun went down. My old self wouldn't consider taking out the trash a major victory for the week. So no, there haven't really been any stretches where I bounced back or felt the fog lift. It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. It's just been a solid year and change of that same, exact nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Before this stretch that started with your divorce and move, had you ever gone through a separate period—at any earlier point in your life—where you felt this same kind of low, heavy, no-energy state for weeks or more, followed by a return to feeling normal again?\n\nPatient: Well, I... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, you try to think back over forty-five years of life and you try to find a precedent for something like this, right? Because you want to tell yourself, oh, I beat this before, I know the playbook, I can do it again. But honestly... no. \n\nI mean, sure, everyone gets tired. When I was getting my bachelor's, I was working nights to pay tuition, studying during the day, barely sleeping. I was exhausted. Bone-tired. And later on, pulling double shifts on the force... you get drained. In my line of work, you see a lot of heavy things, you deal with high-stress situations where you have to be completely dialed in because people are relying on you to keep them safe. You come home from a shift like that and you're wiped out. But that was always a completely different kind of tired. It was a physical tired. I still cared. I still had that drive to get up the next day, put the uniform on, and get back out there. \n\nEven when things got really rough personally, like when my ex-wife and I first started having real problems, before the divorce actually happened... it was incredibly stressful. It kept me up at night, my stomach would be in knots, but I never lost my engine. I never lost that spark that makes you get up and actually participate in your own life. If I had ever had a month, or even a couple of weeks, where I couldn't get off the couch or couldn't bring myself to care about basic hygiene or my responsibilities, they would have noticed at work immediately. You can't hide that kind of thing when you're working protective services. They would have pulled me off the street so fast my head would spin. \n\nSo before this whole mess started a year and two months ago? Never. I've never felt this heavy, empty feeling in my entire life. I was always the guy who pushed through, the guy who stayed busy to fix the problem. This complete lack of... of everything... it's completely new territory for me. I keep searching my memory trying to find a time I felt even a fraction of this, just to make it make sense, but there's nothing. I've never been here before.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had any thoughts of death, or thoughts that you'd be better off not being here?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no. Not really. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around making plans or... or thinking about hurting myself or anything like that. That's just... that's not how I operate. Even with all this, even with the divorce and the move and just feeling like I'm made of lead most days, I haven't gone down that road. \n\nYou know, in my line of work, you see the aftermath of that kind of thing. You see what it does to the people left behind. The families, the kids. I've had to be the one to secure a scene where someone... where someone just couldn't take it anymore. And you stand there in that room, and you see the absolute devastation, the permanence of it. It's a heavy thing to carry, seeing that. And I think, because I've seen it up close, because I know exactly what that looks like and smells like and feels like... it's just not an option in my brain. It's like a door that's permanently locked. \n\nSure, I wake up and I stare at that water stain on the ceiling and I think, \"God, I don't want to do this today. I don't want to drag myself out of this bed and go through the motions of another empty day.\" I definitely feel that. I feel the exhaustion of just existing right now. But wanting to actually be dead? Wanting to end it? No. \n\nI just want... I want the heavy feeling to stop. I want to care about things again. I want to look at my guitar and actually want to pick it up, instead of just seeing it as another thing collecting dust. I want to feel like the guy who used to run drills and organize the neighborhood, you know? I don't want to be gone. I just want to be *me* again. So, no... no thoughts of death. Just thoughts of... of wanting to find my way back, I guess, even if I have no idea how to do that right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow has your sleep been over this past year—are you having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than you used to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Well, my sleep... honestly, it's probably the one thing that hasn't completely gone off the rails, which is kind of surprising when you really think about it. I mean, you'd expect me to be tossing and turning, right? With everything that happened—the divorce, packing up the house, moving into this cramped little rental... you'd think I'd be up all night pacing the floorboards, staring out the window. 

But I actually... I don't really have trouble with it. I get into bed around the same time I always did, maybe ten-thirty or eleven. I still do the exact same routine, you know, check the locks—force of habit from the job, you never really turn off that perimeter-check mindset. I check the front deadbolt, check the back window, make sure the stove is off. Then I lay down. And I don't really lay there for hours agonizing over things or staring at the clock. I just sort of... shut down. It's like my brain is just too exhausted from carrying around all this heavy, empty weight all day. 

And I don't really wake up in the middle of the night either. Sometimes the pipes in this building make this awful clanging noise around 3 AM, like someone's taking a wrench to the radiator in the basement, but even then, I usually just sleep right through it. My ex-wife used to say I could sleep through a freight train driving through the living room

I'll be looking at the wall or the floor, and I know there are things to do. I have a whole list in my head of things I should be doing, basic things like laundry or taking out the trash or getting groceries, but I just can't make myself start. It’s like the connection between my brain saying \"get up\" and my body actually moving is just completely severed. I just sit there while the whole day disappears, and I can't figure out where the time went or where the old me went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s definitely been longer than a few weeks. It’s not one of those things where you just wake up one Tuesday and everything is different, you know? It’s more like... like a slow leak in a tire. You don't notice it at first, but eventually, you're just riding on the rim. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back, I think things really started unraveling around the time I moved into this smaller rental. That was... gosh, that was right after the divorce was finalized. So, we're talking about a year and two months ago, roughly. Give or take a couple of weeks. \n\nMoving is always stressful, right? Especially when it's under those circumstances. You're packing up a whole life, dividing things into boxes, trying to figure out what fits in a place that's half the size of what you're used to. I thought the exhaustion was just from all that. Just the physical toll of hauling furniture up a flight of stairs by myself, because I didn't want to bother anyone to help. And then the emotional toll of it all. I kept telling myself, \"Once the boxes are unpacked, once I get the pictures hung up and find a place for everything, I'll bounce back.\" \n\nBut I never really unpacked everything. There are still boxes sitting in the corner of the bedroom. I just... I ran out of gas. And that was over a year ago. So yeah, it’s been a long time. A year and two months of just... fading out.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year and two months, has your mood and energy stayed pretty much the same low level the whole time, or have there been certain stretches—days or weeks—where you actually felt noticeably better or more like your old self, followed by times it got worse again?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing. I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? Because you'd think, over the course of fourteen months, there'd be at least a weekend where I woke up and thought, \"Hey, I'm going to go tackle that trail today,\" or, \"I'm going to finally restring that acoustic and learn a new song.\" You'd think there'd be a spike, even a small one. \n\nBut honestly, if I'm looking back at the whole stretch since I moved into that rental... it's just been this flat, gray line. It's not like a roller coaster where I have a good week and then crash for a bad week. It's more like I just sank to the bottom of a pool and I've just been walking along the bottom ever since. I mean, sure, maybe there's a Tuesday where I manage to actually take the trash out before it piles up too high, or a Thursday where I microwave a meal instead of just eating cold cereal, but I wouldn't call that feeling *better*. I wouldn't call that feeling like my old self. \n\nMy old self would have been out there organizing a neighborhood watch or running drills with the kids until the sun went down. My old self wouldn't consider taking out the trash a major victory for the week. So no, there haven't really been any stretches where I bounced back or felt the fog lift. It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. It's just been a solid year and change of that same, exact nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Before this stretch that started with your divorce and move, had you ever gone through a separate period—at any earlier point in your life—where you felt this same kind of low, heavy, no-energy state for weeks or more, followed by a return to feeling normal again?\n\nPatient: Well, I... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, you try to think back over forty-five years of life and you try to find a precedent for something like this, right? Because you want to tell yourself, oh, I beat this before, I know the playbook, I can do it again. But honestly... no. \n\nI mean, sure, everyone gets tired. When I was getting my bachelor's, I was working nights to pay tuition, studying during the day, barely sleeping. I was exhausted. Bone-tired. And later on, pulling double shifts on the force... you get drained. In my line of work, you see a lot of heavy things, you deal with high-stress situations where you have to be completely dialed in because people are relying on you to keep them safe. You come home from a shift like that and you're wiped out. But that was always a completely different kind of tired. It was a physical tired. I still cared. I still had that drive to get up the next day, put the uniform on, and get back out there. \n\nEven when things got really rough personally, like when my ex-wife and I first started having real problems, before the divorce actually happened... it was incredibly stressful. It kept me up at night, my stomach would be in knots, but I never lost my engine. I never lost that spark that makes you get up and actually participate in your own life. If I had ever had a month, or even a couple of weeks, where I couldn't get off the couch or couldn't bring myself to care about basic hygiene or my responsibilities, they would have noticed at work immediately. You can't hide that kind of thing when you're working protective services. They would have pulled me off the street so fast my head would spin. \n\nSo before this whole mess started a year and two months ago? Never. I've never felt this heavy, empty feeling in my entire life. I was always the guy who pushed through, the guy who stayed busy to fix the problem. This complete lack of... of everything... it's completely new territory for me. I keep searching my memory trying to find a time I felt even a fraction of this, just to make it make sense, but there's nothing. I've never been here before.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had any thoughts of death, or thoughts that you'd be better off not being here?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no. Not really. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around making plans or... or thinking about hurting myself or anything like that. That's just... that's not how I operate. Even with all this, even with the divorce and the move and just feeling like I'm made of lead most days, I haven't gone down that road. \n\nYou know, in my line of work, you see the aftermath of that kind of thing. You see what it does to the people left behind. The families, the kids. I've had to be the one to secure a scene where someone... where someone just couldn't take it anymore. And you stand there in that room, and you see the absolute devastation, the permanence of it. It's a heavy thing to carry, seeing that. And I think, because I've seen it up close, because I know exactly what that looks like and smells like and feels like... it's just not an option in my brain. It's like a door that's permanently locked. \n\nSure, I wake up and I stare at that water stain on the ceiling and I think, \"God, I don't want to do this today. I don't want to drag myself out of this bed and go through the motions of another empty day.\" I definitely feel that. I feel the exhaustion of just existing right now. But wanting to actually be dead? Wanting to end it? No. \n\nI just want... I want the heavy feeling to stop. I want to care about things again. I want to look at my guitar and actually want to pick it up, instead of just seeing it as another thing collecting dust. I want to feel like the guy who used to run drills and organize the neighborhood, you know? I don't want to be gone. I just want to be *me* again. So, no... no thoughts of death. Just thoughts of... of wanting to find my way back, I guess, even if I have no idea how to do that right now.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year—are you having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep... honestly, it's probably the one thing that hasn't completely gone off the rails, which is kind of surprising when you really think about it. I mean, you'd expect me to be tossing and turning, right? With everything that happened—the divorce, packing up the house, moving into this cramped little rental... you'd think I'd be up all night pacing the floorboards, staring out the window. \n\nBut I actually... I don't really have trouble with it. I get into bed around the same time I always did, maybe ten-thirty or eleven. I still do the exact same routine, you know, check the locks—force of habit from the job, you never really turn off that perimeter-check mindset. I check the front deadbolt, check the back window, make sure the stove is off. Then I lay down. And I don't really lay there for hours agonizing over things or staring at the clock. I just sort of... shut down. It's like my brain is just too exhausted from carrying around all this heavy, empty weight all day. \n\nAnd I don't really wake up in the middle of the night either. Sometimes the pipes in this building make this awful clanging noise around 3 AM, like someone's taking a wrench to the radiator in the basement, but even then, I usually just sleep right through it. My ex-wife used to say I could sleep through a freight train driving through the living room\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my appetite... you know, it's funny you ask that, because I was just thinking about this the other day when I was staring into the fridge. No, honestly, I haven't really noticed any big changes. I mean, I'm not exactly cooking up a storm these days. When we were married, we used to do these big Sunday dinners, you know? Roast a chicken, make mashed potatoes from scratch, the whole nine yards. I used to love manning the grill in the summer, too. We had this big backyard at the old house, and I'd be out there with the tongs, flipping burgers, chatting with the neighbors over the fence. 

Now, in this apartment, I barely even look at the stove. But it's not because I'm not hungry, or because I'm eating too much, it's just... the effort of it. So I eat a lot of sandwiches. Turkey and Swiss on whole wheat, usually. Maybe some soup from a can. It gets the job done. I still eat three meals a day, pretty much like clockwork. Breakfast is usually just toast and coffee—black, the way I've always taken it since my first year on the force. You learn pretty quick that you don't have time to mess around with cream and sugar when you're grabbing a cup from a gas station at 4 AM. 

And my weight? I haven't really stepped on a scale recently. I don't own one anymore; we left it at the house when we split the stuff up. But my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did a year ago. I have to wear the tactical trousers, the ones with the cargo pockets, and they have this reinforced waistband that has absolutely zero give to it. If you gain or lose even five pounds, you feel it immediately when you try to snap that button. And they still fit perfectly. I haven't had to adjust my belt by even a single notch. 

So, yeah, I guess you could say my eating habits are a lot more boring now, a lot more utilitarian, just putting fuel in the tank so the engine doesn't stall out. But the actual amount I'm eating, and my weight? No, that hasn't changed at all. It's just steady. Like the sleep. Just another thing that's somehow staying normal while everything else feels so incredibly heavy.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome in. Before we begin, can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... I guess the short answer is that mostly, I just don't feel like myself anymore. But it’s not really a short answer kind of thing, because it’s just been this heavy, slow change. I used to be a guy who was always moving. You know, you get used to a certain pace in my line of work, always being on alert, always doing something. And outside of that, I was really active. I’d go hiking up the ridge every single weekend, rain or shine, just to clear my head. I was coaching a youth league, which takes a ton of energy, planning the drills and talking to the parents. I’d come home and play my guitar—this old acoustic I’ve had since I got my bachelor's—just to unwind. \n\nBut now... I don't know. I just stopped. I stopped hiking completely. I dropped out of coaching, didn't even really give them a good excuse. I haven't picked up the guitar in months, maybe longer, the strings are probably dead by now. I don't even call people back anymore. My phone will buzz, and I’ll look at the name, and I just... I can't do it. I just let it ring and tell myself I'll do it later, but I never do. \n\nAnd the worst part is, some days I just sit in that small rental of mine for hours. Just sitting there in the quiet. I'll be looking at the wall or the floor, and I know there are things to do. I have a whole list in my head of things I should be doing, basic things like laundry or taking out the trash or getting groceries, but I just can't make myself start. It’s like the connection between my brain saying \"get up\" and my body actually moving is just completely severed. I just sit there while the whole day disappears, and I can't figure out where the time went or where the old me went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s definitely been longer than a few weeks. It’s not one of those things where you just wake up one Tuesday and everything is different, you know? It’s more like... like a slow leak in a tire. You don't notice it at first, but eventually, you're just riding on the rim. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back, I think things really started unraveling around the time I moved into this smaller rental. That was... gosh, that was right after the divorce was finalized. So, we're talking about a year and two months ago, roughly. Give or take a couple of weeks. \n\nMoving is always stressful, right? Especially when it's under those circumstances. You're packing up a whole life, dividing things into boxes, trying to figure out what fits in a place that's half the size of what you're used to. I thought the exhaustion was just from all that. Just the physical toll of hauling furniture up a flight of stairs by myself, because I didn't want to bother anyone to help. And then the emotional toll of it all. I kept telling myself, \"Once the boxes are unpacked, once I get the pictures hung up and find a place for everything, I'll bounce back.\" \n\nBut I never really unpacked everything. There are still boxes sitting in the corner of the bedroom. I just... I ran out of gas. And that was over a year ago. So yeah, it’s been a long time. A year and two months of just... fading out.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year and two months, has your mood and energy stayed pretty much the same low level the whole time, or have there been certain stretches—days or weeks—where you actually felt noticeably better or more like your old self, followed by times it got worse again?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing. I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? Because you'd think, over the course of fourteen months, there'd be at least a weekend where I woke up and thought, \"Hey, I'm going to go tackle that trail today,\" or, \"I'm going to finally restring that acoustic and learn a new song.\" You'd think there'd be a spike, even a small one. \n\nBut honestly, if I'm looking back at the whole stretch since I moved into that rental... it's just been this flat, gray line. It's not like a roller coaster where I have a good week and then crash for a bad week. It's more like I just sank to the bottom of a pool and I've just been walking along the bottom ever since. I mean, sure, maybe there's a Tuesday where I manage to actually take the trash out before it piles up too high, or a Thursday where I microwave a meal instead of just eating cold cereal, but I wouldn't call that feeling *better*. I wouldn't call that feeling like my old self. \n\nMy old self would have been out there organizing a neighborhood watch or running drills with the kids until the sun went down. My old self wouldn't consider taking out the trash a major victory for the week. So no, there haven't really been any stretches where I bounced back or felt the fog lift. It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. It's just been a solid year and change of that same, exact nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Before this stretch that started with your divorce and move, had you ever gone through a separate period—at any earlier point in your life—where you felt this same kind of low, heavy, no-energy state for weeks or more, followed by a return to feeling normal again?\n\nPatient: Well, I... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, you try to think back over forty-five years of life and you try to find a precedent for something like this, right? Because you want to tell yourself, oh, I beat this before, I know the playbook, I can do it again. But honestly... no. \n\nI mean, sure, everyone gets tired. When I was getting my bachelor's, I was working nights to pay tuition, studying during the day, barely sleeping. I was exhausted. Bone-tired. And later on, pulling double shifts on the force... you get drained. In my line of work, you see a lot of heavy things, you deal with high-stress situations where you have to be completely dialed in because people are relying on you to keep them safe. You come home from a shift like that and you're wiped out. But that was always a completely different kind of tired. It was a physical tired. I still cared. I still had that drive to get up the next day, put the uniform on, and get back out there. \n\nEven when things got really rough personally, like when my ex-wife and I first started having real problems, before the divorce actually happened... it was incredibly stressful. It kept me up at night, my stomach would be in knots, but I never lost my engine. I never lost that spark that makes you get up and actually participate in your own life. If I had ever had a month, or even a couple of weeks, where I couldn't get off the couch or couldn't bring myself to care about basic hygiene or my responsibilities, they would have noticed at work immediately. You can't hide that kind of thing when you're working protective services. They would have pulled me off the street so fast my head would spin. \n\nSo before this whole mess started a year and two months ago? Never. I've never felt this heavy, empty feeling in my entire life. I was always the guy who pushed through, the guy who stayed busy to fix the problem. This complete lack of... of everything... it's completely new territory for me. I keep searching my memory trying to find a time I felt even a fraction of this, just to make it make sense, but there's nothing. I've never been here before.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had any thoughts of death, or thoughts that you'd be better off not being here?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no. Not really. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around making plans or... or thinking about hurting myself or anything like that. That's just... that's not how I operate. Even with all this, even with the divorce and the move and just feeling like I'm made of lead most days, I haven't gone down that road. \n\nYou know, in my line of work, you see the aftermath of that kind of thing. You see what it does to the people left behind. The families, the kids. I've had to be the one to secure a scene where someone... where someone just couldn't take it anymore. And you stand there in that room, and you see the absolute devastation, the permanence of it. It's a heavy thing to carry, seeing that. And I think, because I've seen it up close, because I know exactly what that looks like and smells like and feels like... it's just not an option in my brain. It's like a door that's permanently locked. \n\nSure, I wake up and I stare at that water stain on the ceiling and I think, \"God, I don't want to do this today. I don't want to drag myself out of this bed and go through the motions of another empty day.\" I definitely feel that. I feel the exhaustion of just existing right now. But wanting to actually be dead? Wanting to end it? No. \n\nI just want... I want the heavy feeling to stop. I want to care about things again. I want to look at my guitar and actually want to pick it up, instead of just seeing it as another thing collecting dust. I want to feel like the guy who used to run drills and organize the neighborhood, you know? I don't want to be gone. I just want to be *me* again. So, no... no thoughts of death. Just thoughts of... of wanting to find my way back, I guess, even if I have no idea how to do that right now.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year—are you having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep... honestly, it's probably the one thing that hasn't completely gone off the rails, which is kind of surprising when you really think about it. I mean, you'd expect me to be tossing and turning, right? With everything that happened—the divorce, packing up the house, moving into this cramped little rental... you'd think I'd be up all night pacing the floorboards, staring out the window. \n\nBut I actually... I don't really have trouble with it. I get into bed around the same time I always did, maybe ten-thirty or eleven. I still do the exact same routine, you know, check the locks—force of habit from the job, you never really turn off that perimeter-check mindset. I check the front deadbolt, check the back window, make sure the stove is off. Then I lay down. And I don't really lay there for hours agonizing over things or staring at the clock. I just sort of... shut down. It's like my brain is just too exhausted from carrying around all this heavy, empty weight all day. \n\nAnd I don't really wake up in the middle of the night either. Sometimes the pipes in this building make this awful clanging noise around 3 AM, like someone's taking a wrench to the radiator in the basement, but even then, I usually just sleep right through it. My ex-wife used to say I could sleep through a freight train driving through the living room\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight—either eating much less or much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... you know, it's funny you ask that, because I was just thinking about this the other day when I was staring into the fridge. No, honestly, I haven't really noticed any big changes. I mean, I'm not exactly cooking up a storm these days. When we were married, we used to do these big Sunday dinners, you know? Roast a chicken, make mashed potatoes from scratch, the whole nine yards. I used to love manning the grill in the summer, too. We had this big backyard at the old house, and I'd be out there with the tongs, flipping burgers, chatting with the neighbors over the fence. \n\nNow, in this apartment, I barely even look at the stove. But it's not because I'm not hungry, or because I'm eating too much, it's just... the effort of it. So I eat a lot of sandwiches. Turkey and Swiss on whole wheat, usually. Maybe some soup from a can. It gets the job done. I still eat three meals a day, pretty much like clockwork. Breakfast is usually just toast and coffee—black, the way I've always taken it since my first year on the force. You learn pretty quick that you don't have time to mess around with cream and sugar when you're grabbing a cup from a gas station at 4 AM. \n\nAnd my weight? I haven't really stepped on a scale recently. I don't own one anymore; we left it at the house when we split the stuff up. But my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did a year ago. I have to wear the tactical trousers, the ones with the cargo pockets, and they have this reinforced waistband that has absolutely zero give to it. If you gain or lose even five pounds, you feel it immediately when you try to snap that button. And they still fit perfectly. I haven't had to adjust my belt by even a single notch. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess you could say my eating habits are a lot more boring now, a lot more utilitarian, just putting fuel in the tank so the engine doesn't stall out. But the actual amount I'm eating, and my weight? No, that hasn't changed at all. It's just steady. Like the sleep. Just another thing that's somehow staying normal while everything else feels so incredibly heavy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Concentration... no, no, that's still intact. You see, when you do what I do for twenty-odd years, focus is just hammered into you. It has to be. If I'm out on shift, or even just doing paperwork—which, let me tell you, is about eighty percent of the job these days, just endless forms and incident reports, making sure every little detail is documented—you have to be sharp. You miss a detail on a report, a case gets thrown out, or worse, someone gets hurt. 

So I can still sit down with a stack of files, you know, the incident logs or the daily briefs, and read through them without my mind wandering off. I mean, sure, I might feel that heavy exhaustion I was telling you about earlier, that feeling like I'm wearing a lead tactical vest all day, but my eyes still track the words perfectly fine. I comprehend exactly what I'm reading. I don't have to read the same paragraph over and over again like some guys do when they start getting burnt out on the job. I used to read a lot more for pleasure, Tom Clancy novels and that sort of thing, back when I had the house and the backyard, but even now, if I pick up a manual or a case file, I'm locked in. 

And decisions? That's... well, that's just muscle memory at this point. It really is. When you're securing a scene, or dealing with a dispute out on the street, you can't stand there and hum and haw and weigh the pros and cons. You have to make a call right then and there. Step left, step right, de-escalate, call for backup. It's instantaneous. Even with the divorce and everything else completely upending my personal life, when I put the uniform on, the decision-making part of my brain just clicks right into gear like it always has. 

I was actually just thinking about this when I was filling out a log yesterday. I had to detail this whole sequence of events for a minor incident—just a standard property damage call, guy backed his truck into a commercial loading dock—and I realized I was recalling the exact timeline, the exact placement of the vehicles, making the call on how to code it without any hesitation at all. So the focus is definitely there. The cognitive machinery, if you want to call it that, is all still running exactly the way it's supposed to. It's just the motivation behind it that feels hollowed out. But the actual ability to focus on a task, or decide what to do next? No, I haven't noticed any changes there. That hasn't slipped at all. Which is lucky, honestly, because if I lost that, they'd probably have to take my badge, and then I really wouldn't know what to do with myself.

I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? Because you'd think, over the course of fourteen months, there'd be at least a weekend where I woke up and thought, \"Hey, I'm going to go tackle that trail today,\" or, \"I'm going to finally restring that acoustic and learn a new song.\" You'd think there'd be a spike, even a small one. \n\nBut honestly, if I'm looking back at the whole stretch since I moved into that rental... it's just been this flat, gray line. But it's not because I'm not hungry, or because I'm eating too much, it's just... the effort of it. So I eat a lot of sandwiches. Turkey and Swiss on whole wheat, usually. Maybe some soup from a can. It gets the job done. I still eat three meals a day, pretty much like clockwork. Breakfast is usually just toast and coffee—black, the way I've always taken it since my first year on the force. You learn pretty quick that you don't have time to mess around with cream and sugar when you're grabbing a cup from a gas station at 4 AM. \n\nAnd my weight? I haven't really stepped on a scale recently. I don't own one anymore; we left it at the house when we split the stuff up. But my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did a year ago. I have to wear the tactical trousers, the ones with the cargo pockets, and they have this reinforced waistband that has absolutely zero give to it. If you gain or lose even five pounds, you feel it immediately when you try to snap that button. And they still fit perfectly. I haven't had to adjust my belt by even a single notch. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess you could say my eating habits are a lot more boring now, a lot more utilitarian, just putting fuel in the tank so the engine doesn't stall out. But the actual amount I'm eating, and my weight? No, that hasn't changed at all. It's just steady. Like the sleep. Just another thing that's somehow staying normal while everything else feels so incredibly heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions, like trouble focusing on tasks or reading, over this past year?\n\nPatient: Concentration... no, no, that's still intact. You see, when you do what I do for twenty-odd years, focus is just hammered into you. It has to be. If I'm out on shift, or even just doing paperwork—which, let me tell you, is about eighty percent of the job these days, just endless forms and incident reports, making sure every little detail is documented—you have to be sharp. You miss a detail on a report, a case gets thrown out, or worse, someone gets hurt. \n\nSo I can still sit down with a stack of files, you know, the incident logs or the daily briefs, and read through them without my mind wandering off. I mean, sure, I might feel that heavy exhaustion I was telling you about earlier, that feeling like I'm wearing a lead tactical vest all day, but my eyes still track the words perfectly fine. I comprehend exactly what I'm reading. I don't have to read the same paragraph over and over again like some guys do when they start getting burnt out on the job. I used to read a lot more for pleasure, Tom Clancy novels and that sort of thing, back when I had the house and the backyard, but even now, if I pick up a manual or a case file, I'm locked in. \n\nAnd decisions? That's... well, that's just muscle memory at this point. It really is. When you're securing a scene, or dealing with a dispute out on the street, you can't stand there and hum and haw and weigh the pros and cons. You have to make a call right then and there. Step left, step right, de-escalate, call for backup. It's instantaneous. Even with the divorce and everything else completely upending my personal life, when I put the uniform on, the decision-making part of my brain just clicks right into gear like it always has. \n\nI was actually just thinking about this when I was filling out a log yesterday. I had to detail this whole sequence of events for a minor incident—just a standard property damage call, guy backed his truck into a commercial loading dock—and I realized I was recalling the exact timeline, the exact placement of the vehicles, making the call on how to code it without any hesitation at all. So the focus is definitely there. The cognitive machinery, if you want to call it that, is all still running exactly the way it's supposed to. It's just the motivation behind it that feels hollowed out. But the actual ability to focus on a task, or decide what to do next? No, I haven't noticed any changes there. That hasn't slipped at all. Which is lucky, honestly, because if I lost that, they'd probably have to take my badge, and then I really wouldn't know what to do with myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Moving slowly? Or... fidgeting? No, I... honestly, if I was doing either of those things, I wouldn't hear the end of it from the guys at the precinct. 

You see, in my line of work, your body language is basically your resume, day in and day out. If you're out on the street and you're sluggish, or if your reaction time is off by even a half-second, that's a massive liability. My shift sergeant, Miller—he's this old-school guy, been on the force since the late nineties, eagle eyes, doesn't miss a single thing—if he saw me dragging my feet during roll call or taking too long to get out of the cruiser, he'd pull me aside instantly. He's the kind of guy who will call you out in front of the whole squad if your boots aren't shined right, let alone if you're moving like molasses. He'd be in my face saying, you know, asking if I need a coffee or a desk duty shift. But he hasn't said a word to me about my pace. Nobody has.

And as for being restless, or jittery... no, that's just not how I'm built, and it's definitely not how I was trained. When you spend years standing on perimeters—you know, guarding a scene where you just have to hold the line and keep the public back while the detectives or the fire crews do their thing inside—you learn how to be a statue. I remember this one time, back in maybe 2018, we had this massive warehouse fire in the dead of winter, and I was posted at the outer cordon for six hours. Six straight hours, freezing rain, wind cutting right through the high-vis jacket. You don't fidget. You just plant your feet, rest your hands on your belt—right above the radio, exactly like they drill into you at the academy—and you just lock in. You conserve your energy. 

If you're bouncing around, shifting your weight constantly, tapping your foot, people notice that. Civilians notice, and it makes them nervous. They look at you and they think, "Why is that officer so jumpy? What's he looking at that I'm not seeing? Is something about to happen?" It escalates the whole energy of the street. So you just train it out of yourself. You learn to just stand your ground. 

So, no, nobody has said anything about me moving differently. I still walk the exact same way, patrol the exact same way. I might feel... well, like I was telling you earlier, I feel that heavy weight, that sort of hollowed-out exhaustion on the inside, but on the outside? The chassis is moving exactly the same as it always has. No slower, no faster. Just the standard patrol pace. You just... you just put one foot in front of the other, same as always.

They look at you and they think, \"Why is that officer so jumpy? What's he looking at that I'm not seeing? Is something about to happen?\" It escalates the whole energy of the street. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. I keep trying to map it out in my head, trying to find some kind of pattern or some break in the clouds, you know? It's not like a roller coaster where I have a good week and then crash for a bad week. It's more like I just sank to the bottom of a pool and I've just been walking along the bottom ever since. I mean, sure, maybe there's a Tuesday where I manage to actually take the trash out before it piles up too high, or a Thursday where I microwave a meal instead of just eating cold cereal, but I wouldn't call that feeling *better*. I wouldn't call that feeling like my old self. \n\nMy old self would have been out there organizing a neighborhood watch or running drills with the kids until the sun went down. My old self wouldn't consider taking out the trash a major victory for the week. So no, there haven't really been any stretches where I bounced back or felt the fog lift. It's just been this constant, steady weight. Every single day, I wake up, and it's the exact same heavy feeling. I just stare at the ceiling, looking at the water stain near the vent, and I know exactly how the rest of the day is going to go. It's just been a solid year and change of that same, exact nothingness.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
